The texture is NEAREST, no mipmaps, no premultiply; its node is `mergeable = false`.

**Cover kinds are data** (`kinds.ts`, `COVER_KINDS`, ids 0 to 15). A `CoverKind` has `maxH` (the kind's maximum height in art px: grass 4, moss 2; `GH_MAX` = 3 is the largest `gh` height factor, 0.25 to 3 in params, which the chunk bounds assume), `taper`, `climb`, `creep`, `rough` (the rim), `sway` (tiles at the top sheet), `footShade`, `darken` (the ground's colour factor under it) and `bushels` (a `BushelLook` or null). The shaders read these through uniform tables (`coverRead.ts`: `kindLook`, `bushelTone`, `bushelSwayOf`), so no shader branches on a kind. **To add one** (snow, say): add one entry to `COVER_KINDS` (the next free id), and a source (below) that fills its masks; the mesh, the shaders and the tests that loop over `COVER_KINDS` need no change. Grass: 4 px, taper 5, one px of height per 3 px from the rim, creeps 3.5 px over its rim, sway 0.06, bushels. Moss: 2 px, no sway, no bushels. All the numbers are first guesses except the approved ones (grass heights and taper, sway, bushel sizes).

**Heights** (`heights.ts`, `HeightBuilder`). A source gives two masks per art pixel: `grow` (cover stands here) and `allow` (it may reach over a rim, where the cover may go). A two-pass chamfer distance field measures how far a pixel is inside `grow` or outside it; inside, the height climbs by 1 px per `climb` px of distance up to `maxH` over the first `taper` px, with noise (hash of the pixel and the seed); outside, over the rim up to `creep` px, only where `allow` is set. The field is built in bands of rows so it can run in a time-sliced job.

**The three sources** (`CoverSource`: kind, size, `grow`, `allow`, `colour`, `prep` units, optional `density`, `spots`, `bare`).
- *Woodland* (`woodland.ts`): grows on Floor, Trunk and Thicket tiles (the painter's grass and thicket kinds, `kindRows` in `game/woodland/ground.ts`), may reach over every ground kind but the void, so paths are bare ground. Two `GroundPainter` pictures: the bare one (grass tiles as road, the picture of the ground) and the cover one (roads as grass, the colours of the cover, so the cover has grass colour where it creeps over a path). The thicket has four times the bushel density.
- *Cove* (`cove.ts`): `coveGrass` of `game/covemath.ts` is `grow`, the Floor tiles of the room are `allow`, the colours are the carpet's (`paintCove`; its partly transparent stone shadows are skipped: the 3D view casts its own). The tufts and ferns come in as `spots` (`Cove3d.bushelSpots()`).
- *Moss* (`moss.ts`): in a dungeon without lava, on Floor tiles with no door, lava or chasm within one tile and not in a corridor (walls on both opposite sides), not in the cove. **Waterside** (#324): a Floor tile with water among its eight neighbours is always mossy, whatever the area noise says and in a corridor too; moss grows there from the water's edge (`waterReach` 9 px, with the wobble), so every pool has a mossy rim. Inside the pool's rounded outline the ground falls into the water (`dips/shape.ts`), so pixels where the outline's mask (`maskAt` of water) reaches `waterEdge` 0.42 neither grow moss nor let it creep (`allow` 0). Water tiles themselves carry none. `ignoreLava` (the `?r3d=liquids` page, which shows every terrain in one room) grows moss on a map with lava. A low-frequency noise (`MOSS.area`, `areaCut`) picks the mossy areas (some rooms, not all); moss then grows from corners (`cornerReach` 12 px) and wall feet (`wallReach` 6 px) with a wobbling edge, plus rare free patches (ellipses) inside mossy tiles. The seed is `tilesSeed`: FNV-1a over the solid/open layout only (one byte per tile, `heightAt > 0`). Doors, water and lava change during a run and do not count, so every party member and a view rebuilt after a reconnect get the same moss; two runs with the same wall layout would share it (practically never). Eligibility reads the tiles as given. A pool that appears during a run gives a client that joins or rebuilds later a full mossy rim (the Floor tiles beside it become waterside), which a client that was there from the start lacks, so the two differ along the whole pool. `mossSource` returns null when any Lava tile exists, so a client that builds its cover after lava appeared mid-run (a reconnect) shows no moss at all: a known edge. The dev scene (`?r3d=demo`) passes `everywhere` to make every room mossy.

**The job** (`coverJob.ts`). `coverSources(map, info, opts)` picks the sources: the woodland's, or a dungeon's moss followed by the cove's grass (the later source wins where it has height). `coverJob(sources, seed)` returns a `CoverJob`: every source's `prep` units, then every source's height units, then, per source, units that write its pixels into the image in bands of 16 rows and one that places its bushels (the cove source also does its colour and masks in `prep` bands, over its rectangle only); when the last unit has run the job drops its units, builders and sources and keeps only the result; `step(budgetMs)` works at most `COVER_BUDGET_MS` (4 ms) per frame and at least one unit. `result()` gives a `CoverResult { image, bare, bushels, sheets }` once done: `bare` is the woodland's bare ground picture (null elsewhere), `sheets` the tallest kind's `maxH` (how many sheets are drawn). The stages (`runStage.ts`, `woodlandStage.ts`, `coveStage.ts`, `demoStage.ts`) run the job and keep the result for the rebuilds after a lost device.

**Chunk meshes and bounds** (`coverMesh.ts`, `coverLayer.ts`). `buildCoverChunk(result, cx, cz)` cuts the image into the map's 16 by 16 tile chunks (`CHUNK`) and returns the sheet quads (only cells that have cover; sheet `n` only where some pixel reaches `n`; attributes `position`, `layer`) and the bushel cards (6 vertices each: foot, corner, info = width, height, seed, fern) of the chunk, or null. The bounds hold the tallest sheet at `GH_MAX`, the bushels' half width, their sway, the stretch of the cards by 1 / cos(`PITCH_MAX`) and `CARD_BIAS`, because the raw positions lack the sway and the push toward the camera. `CoverLayer` makes one mesh per chunk and kind of geometry (sheet render order 0.1, bushel 0.15, before the trees' depth pass), culled by those bounds; it owns the two materials and the texture.

**The sheet shader** (`coverNodes.ts`, `coverSheetMaterial`). Vertices never move in x or z. The colour node reads the image at the cell's pixel: the cell's kind sets the lean, `swayNode` (the woodland's wind, gust included) gives a lean of `(layer / maxH)^2 * kind.sway` tiles, and the pixel upwind of that lean is the one drawn (the texel read gives height, colour and kind), so low grass stirs without a vertex moving. The colour is the pixel's, darker toward the foot (`footShade` rising to 1 at the top), lit like the floor (`lightAt` at the art pixel's centre, so one pixel is one colour; the sun term as the floor's, wrap 0.35), then the sight step (`Sight.unseenAt`, `apply`: misted, never hidden on daylight maps). Alpha is 1 where the height reaches the sheet, tested at 0.5; double sided.

**The bushels** (`bushels.ts`, `placeBushels`; shader `bushelMaterial`). Tall-grass cards stand in clusters where the cover is 2 px or more (the bushel foot also needs height under it and 0.4 tile to each side): 7 to 12 px wide and 7 to 13 px high, ferns 10 to 14 by 5 to 8. The cluster count follows the thick area weighted by the source's `density`, and the cluster centres are picked weighted by that density too (so the thicket band is the densest and tallest). The spots (the cove's tufts and ferns) add clusters at their feet. Placement uses a seeded generator, never `Math.random`. The card faces the camera like the other cards (`CARD_BIAS` push, stretch by 1 / cos(pitch)); the blades are made in the shader per pixel column from the seed (`hash3` of `noiseNodes.ts`, shared with the trees), in three tones from the kind's `bushels.tones`; sway is `oy^2 * 0.22` tiles; the sight stipples it at the foot like other scenery.

**Lifts** (`coverField.ts`, `CoverField`). What lies flat on the ground would sink into the cover: ground shadows, lying bodies, skulls, landed butterflies, fallen leaves. `topAt(x, z, r, gh)` returns the largest cover height (tiles) at the centre and four points at distance `r`, times `gh`; the stages hand it to `Figures3d` and `Critters3d` (`ground`), cards lift by their `base` (`cards.ts`), and the falling leaf reads `coverHeightNode` in the shader. Without cover, or with the layer off, the lift is 0. The ground itself darkens under standing cover by the kind's `darken` (`worldMaterial`); with the layer off it shows the cover's colour flat.

**Parameters and panel.** `cover` (URL `cover=0` turns it off; default on) and `gh` (`0.25` to `3`, default 1; the height factor, clamped to `GH_MAX` on the way to the shader). Quality does not touch the cover: High, Balanced and Fast draw the same. The panel has the switch "Ground cover" and a slider for `gh`; `window.__r3d.cover` holds `{ sheets, bushels }` once the job is done.

**Failure modes.** A cover image larger than the device's texture limit throws `GroundTooLargeError` (`groundError.ts`, re-exported by `view.ts`): the layer stays off with the stand-in texture, the host's error box shows the message, and `RunView3d` lifts nothing. After a lost device the new view gets the kept `CoverResult` again (the layer is rebuilt and the texture uploaded again, nothing is recomputed). A result with no cover (a dungeon without moss or cove, a 1x1 image) builds no mesh.

**Speed.** Measured 2026-11-14 on the dev notebook's RTX 3060 (headed Brave with the start line of `tools/e2e/gpu.mjs`, uncapped, 2560 x 1280, zoom 8, the dev pages `?r3d=woodland` (4 sheets, 487 bushels) and `?r3d=demo` with every room mossy (2 sheets)); mean fps / median frame time, cover on vs `cover=0`:

| | woodland High | woodland Fast | mossy demo High | mossy demo Fast |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| WebGPU | 208 / 2.2 ms vs 212 / 2.3 ms | 464 / 1.9 ms vs 468 / 1.9 ms | 540 / 1.5 ms vs 537 / 1.6 ms | 552 / 1.6 ms vs 553 / 1.6 ms |
| WebGL2 | 228 / 4.2 ms vs 346 / 1.9 ms | 612 / 1.5 ms vs 604 / 1.5 ms | 603 / 1.6 ms vs 719 / 1.1 ms | 1298 / 0.5 ms vs 1298 / 0.5 ms |

Every case stays far above 60 fps, so Fast keeps its bushels. The clearest cost is WebGL2 on High in the woodland (median 1.9 → 4.2 ms). `cover=0` still draws the meshes and discards every pixel, so these figures understate the layer's cost a little compared with no layer at all. The woodland on WebGPU High has 1 % lows of 14 fps with the cover on and off alike: those stalls are not the cover's.

**Tests.** In `render3d/cover/`: `kinds.test.ts` (the encoding, each kind at its own id, moss lower than grass without sway or bushels), `heights.test.ts` (the taper, the creep, only where `allow`, determinism), `moss.test.ts` (eligibility, corridors, a waterside corridor tile beside ShallowWater and DeepWater, doors and hazards, the seed from the wall layout only, `everywhere`, none with lava), `woodland.test.ts` (grass tiles, bare ground under paths, the two pictures), `cove.test.ts` (the carpet's shape and colours, the spots), `bushels.test.ts` (the rules, determinism, density weighting, spots), `coverJob.test.ts` (the budget, thin slices give the same bytes as one step, the spawn row first, the sheets and bare picture of a woodland, a dungeon with and without cove or lava), `coverMesh.test.ts` (quads per sheet, bounds hold the sway and the push, chunk borders), `coverLayer.test.ts` (one sheet mesh and at most one bushel mesh per chunk, the too-large image, a second set replaces the first, the layer stays off without a good image, `gh` clamped, dispose), `coverNodes.test.ts` and `coverRead.test.ts` (the materials build in GLSL and WGSL, the kind tables), `coverField.test.ts` (`topAt`, `gh` clamp). Beside it: additions to `params.test.ts`, `panel.test.ts`, `materials.test.ts`, `sightMaterials.test.ts`, `figures.test.ts`, `critters3d.test.ts`, `cards.test.ts`, `cove3d.test.ts`, `coveStage.test.ts`, `demoStage.test.ts`, `woodlandStage.test.ts`, `runStage.test.ts` and `runView3d.test.ts`.

### The air in 3D (#307)

The dungeon's air (dust, smoke, embers, haze, drips, sunlight through ceiling cracks), plus pollen in the woodland. Look only: no server or protocol change. The design and the plan are on issue #307. Code: `render3d/air/`, the two dev pages `moodStage.ts` and `sunlightStage.ts`. Dungeon (the cove included): cracks, shafts, dust, smoke, embers, haze, drips. Woodland: pollen only. A dungeon without Daylight lights runs no shaft pass.

**Cracks** (`air/cracks.ts`). A Daylight light becomes a crack: an opening in a ceiling plane. `crackOf(light, base, t)` turns a static light and the sun look (`lookFor(id, base)`, `cloudAt` of `game/sunmath.ts`; `base` is the shared `sunLook` in a run, the page's own `SunLook` on `?r3d=sunlight`) into a `Crack`: the patch centre (the light's tile centre), the ceiling height (`look.height`), the unit vector toward the sun (`dir`, from `look.dir * look.slant / look.height`, 1, and `CRACK_Z_TILT`), the opening's ellipse (`rx = patch / 2 / 0.81`, so the rim's middle sits at the patch's edge; `rz = rx * 0.65`; a turn hashed from the id), `widen` (0.5 or 1: the opening's size at the ceiling against the floor's), the colour (`lightRgb(Daylight, colour, look.glow * cloud)`, so a cloud dims it), `main` (the faint main beam, `MAIN_ALPHA`) and the rays as bands of the opening (floor and top edges, from `rayShapes`). `crackMask(crack, x, y, z)` is the light share at a world point: it follows the sun ray down to the floor, divides by the ellipse scaled by the height, and shades it with the demo's wobbly rim, the rays or the main beam, and a streak; 0 above the ceiling and below the floor. `selectCracks(field, footprint, mask, CRACK_CAP, look?)` picks the Daylight lights worth a slot (on, near the view grown by the slant, a tile and `HEIGHT_RANGE[1] / tan(PITCH_MIN)`, the reach of a shaft along the flattest camera, and **only if their own tile is seen**, so no crack shows what the hero cannot see). `packCracks` lays them out as the uniform arrays of the shader (`CRACK_CAP` = 8 slots, `RAY_CAP` = 3 rays). A run with the work light (`light=work`) and a woodland have no cracks.

**The patch is a light term.** `air/crackNodes.ts` is the shader twin of `crackMask` (only `If`/`Loop` flow and no texture read, so it may sit in any branch); `lightAt` in `lightNodes.ts` adds it in a loop `cs` over the cracks, shadowed by walls and cards on the sun ray (`sunRayVis`). The floor patch, the walls and figures it falls on are therefore lit by the same mask the shafts use. The light list keeps a Daylight light too, but only as the **bounce fill**: `staticCands` puts it at `BOUNCE_Y` with `BOUNCE_GAIN` of its brightness (a faint light that the patch "bounces" into the room), dimmed by the crack's cloud.

**The shaft pass** (`air/shafts.ts` numbers and CPU twins, `air/shaftNodes.ts` shaders, `view.ts` `shaftsFor`). Runs only when `shafts` is on and at least one crack was sent (`shaftPlan`). Three renders: (1) a depth pre-pass of the opaque scene at the art size (the picture's camera on layer 0 only; the air meshes sit on `AIR_SKIP_LAYER` so they are not in it), (2) a full-screen march into a small half-float target, one pixel per art pixel: from each surface point (rebuilt from the depth; where nothing was drawn, the point on the ground) toward the camera up to the highest ceiling, `SHAFT_STEPS` (14) samples, each summing every crack's mask times the crack's colour (times the sun ray's visibility when wall shadows are on) with a per-pixel interleaved-gradient offset that never changes with time (no grain, no flicker), then times `SHAFT_GAIN`, masked per pixel at the surface by the sight mask (the shafts are gone where the hero cannot see the surface); (3) the additive composite over the picture through the demo's 5-tap blur (`BLUR_CENTRE` + 4 `BLUR_DIAG`). A figure in the shaft cuts a gap into it, because the march starts at the depth of the figure. The loops are named `sm` (march) and `ss` (cracks inside the march) next to `cs`. The crack uniforms fit WebGPU's buffer limit (the packed arrays are `vec4`s). `readback` lays the last frame's shafts over its picture, so a WebGPU frame can be inspected (`READBACK=1`).

**The simulation** (`air/air.ts`, `Air3d`, no three.js and no GPU; in world tiles with height; the px values of `game/moodmath.ts` convert by /16). `Air3d.step(input)` advances the air by `dt` and returns the frame's `AirParts` (reused arrays: specks, embers, puffs, drops, and `landed`, the spots where a drip reached the water in this step). It owns the `Wind` of `game/wind.ts` (px units: x = tiles * 16, z * 16), which the movers stir and the draught moves; `mapChanged()` re-reads the lava and water tiles. Parts: `dust.ts` (clouds of motes that wander, ride the air, are stirred apart, fade and respawn; a speck has no colour of its own, the shader lights it, and `airLight.ts` estimates the light only to choose where a cloud may spawn, with the cracks' patches and shafts counting as light), `puffs.ts` (torch smoke in gusts at the flame's top, lava smoke and sparks, fire trails and the blast burst, `BurstOnce`), `drips.ts` (a drop falls from `DRIP_TOP` into the pool and leaves two splash pixels; since #345 it draws no rings of its own: its landing is reported in `landed` and becomes a ripple of the water's surface). Movers and shots come from the frame: `game/airmovers.ts` (`MoverTracker`, `collectAirMovers`: figures, server shots, the own predicted shots) and the snapshot's moving lights; `game/airshots.ts` gives each projectile its wake width and heat. In the woodland (`kinds: 'pollen'`) only dust runs, as pollen: the same clouds, drifting with the wind at `POLLEN_DRIFT` (`runView3d.ts`; no drift when the wind is off). Everything the hero cannot see is not simulated into view: dust spawns only on seen tiles, the movers and lava must be seen, and every air quad reads the sight mask when drawn.

**Drawing** (`air/airQuads.ts`, `air/airNodes.ts`, `view.ts`). Camera-facing quads in two dynamic meshes (`airAdd`: specks and embers, additive; `airBlend`: smoke, drops, splash pixels and rings, blended) built from `AirParts` (six vertices per quad: `position`, `corner`, `size`, `rgba`, `kind`, `sightXZ`; a ring lies flat on the water), and the haze as two lit noise sheets (`hazeDyn`, `HAZE_Y` heights, the `HAZE` scale and drift, alphas 0.3 and 0.21 and the colour `HAZE_RGB` 0xd0c4b4, after the play-test found alphas 0.1 and 0.07 in 0x8a7c6c barely visible). Specks and smoke are **lit** (`lightAt`, scene minus ambient for a speck, so dust shows only in light); embers carry their own colour; the haze takes `HAZE_WRAP` of the light with **the wall test only**: it is lit by `lightAt(..., { shadow: 'walls' })`, which keeps each light's wall visibility (`ws` walk, so a light behind a wall never tints the seen side, the anti-goal "nothing in the air reveals the unseen") and leaves out the card and sun-ray walks and the darkvision walk (`shadow: 'full' | 'walls' | 'none'` is a build-time option of `lightNodes.ts`, default `'full'`; the loops `li`, `ws` and `cs` stay). The haze is two sheets over the whole view, so the fully shadowed `lightAt` made the software-rendered dungeon run too slow (`r3d-run.mjs`: the hero moved 0.8 to 1.2 tiles in 2 s with it, 2.1 to 3.1 without the haze). Every material ends with the sight step (the point's own xz, or the quad's `sightXZ` for smoke). Render order: haze (3.4), blended air (3.5), additive air (4.5) (`AIR_ORDER`). A speck is one art pixel (`SPECK`).

**Parameters, panel, F3.** `shafts`, `dust`, `smoke`, `embers`, `haze`, `drips` (switches, on by default; URL `key=0`), `stir` and `draft` (the movers' push and the ambient draught; on by default, shown in the panel only on the mood page through `Host.setAirDev(true)`), `respawn` (seconds a stirred dust cloud waits before it respawns, 0 to 10, default 0; dev pages, not in the panel). The panel's Air group has the six switches. F3 has two new laps: `air` (the simulation, an update system) and `shafts` (the pass, a render system; its depth pre-pass, march and composite).

**A lost device.** `Air3d` belongs to the run (`RunView3d`), not to the view, so the clouds and puffs survive a rebuild. While the view is rebuilt after a device loss (`c.view` is null) the stage does not step the air: **the air pauses**, by design, and goes on when the new view is there. The two dev pages behave the same.

**The work light.** With `light=work` (the panel's Game light off) there are no game lights, so the list is empty and the sun cracks are not sent: specks (lit minus ambient) may not show, and there are no shafts. That is the work light, not a bug.

**The dev pages.**
- `?r3d=mood` (`moodStage.ts`): the room and script of `game/moodDemoScript.ts` (the map, four warm torches, the figures, the knight's loop and dash, the arrow every 3 s, the fireball every 4 s that explodes at the east wall) with the real air: the stage feeds `Air3d` as `RunView3d` does (the torch entities as torches, the moving lights as shots, the knight, arrow and fireball tracked by a `MoverTracker` as movers). Free camera on WASD, zoom 2 unless the URL picks one. The `wind` key is the woodland's sway. The still figures are entities (elf, wizard, skeleton, chest, crate as a barrel); the skull has no entity and is left out.
- `?r3d=sunlight` (`sunlightStage.ts`): the room and fixtures of `game/sunlightDemoScript.ts` (the Daylight crack at `SUN`, a knight in the patch and one beside it, the dead torch of the room and the lit one of the alcove) with dust and haze only (no smoke, embers or drips). The page keeps its own `SunLook`, parsed from the URL by `parseSunLook` (not the shared `sunLook`). Keys: **2** second crack (at `SECOND`), **R** reroll (the crack ids become 1 + 1000 n and 3 + 1000 n, so new looks), **V** vary on/off; the panel shows them as the buttons Second crack, Reroll and Vary (`Host.setPageButtons`, a row of page buttons that are not params; a key calls `syncPageButtons`). URL: `second=1`, `vary=0` and, with `vary=0`, `shape`, `patch`, `slant`, `from`, `clouds`, `often`, `height`, `style`, `rays`, `glow`; `shafts=0` turns the shafts off.

**First guesses to judge** (named constants; the balancing of looks, not the balancing pass): `BOUNCE_GAIN` 0.35 (`lights.ts`), the crack sizes (`RIM_MID` 0.81, `RZ_SHARE` 0.65, `RIM_LO` / `RIM_HI` in `cracks.ts`), `CRACK_Z_TILT` -0.2, `SHAFT_GAIN` 0.11 and `SHAFT_STEPS` 14 (`shafts.ts`), the speck size (`SPECK` = 1 art px) and the dust's counts and heights (`DUST_Y0`, `DUST_YSPAN`, `DUST_TOP`, `CLOUD_R0` / `CLOUD_R1` in `air/dust.ts`, `MAX_CLOUDS` of `game/moodmath.ts`), `DUST_GAIN` 1.3 (`airNodes.ts`), the haze (`HAZE_Y` 0.35 and 0.9, `HAZE_WRAP` 0.35, `HAZE_TEX_PX`, `HAZE_LO` / `HAZE_HI`), `DRIP_TOP` 3, `POLLEN_DRIFT` 0.25 (`runView3d.ts`), the smoke's start height (`SMOKE_STAND` 0.15, `SMOKE_LIFT` 0.3) and the blast burst (`BLAST_Y`, `BLAST_EMBERS`).

**Speed.** Measured 2026-11-14 on the dev notebook's RTX 3060 (headed Brave with the start line of `tools/e2e/gpu.mjs`, 1280 x 768, High, zoom 8; the mean of 12 F3 samples after 4 s; the headed window is not uncapped, so the frame rate is not read from these runs):

| | `shafts` lap | `air` lap |
|---|---|---|
| `?r3d=sunlight` WebGPU | 2.15 ms | 0.25 ms |
| `?r3d=sunlight` WebGL2 | 2.83 ms | 0.30 ms |
| `?r3d=mood` WebGPU (no crack, no pass) | 0 | 1.02 ms |
| `?r3d=mood` WebGL2 | 0 | 0.78 ms |

The laps are the CPU time of the calls; a GPU runs the passes later. Frame rate, same machine and start line with `GPU=uncapped` (the F3 FPS figure; the headed window's frame ms is not meaningful), High, zoom 8, WebGPU, the air on against `&haze=0&shafts=0&dust=0&smoke=0&embers=0&drips=0`: before the cheap haze `?r3d=mood` 733 against 1077 fps and `?r3d=sunlight` 626 against 1166; after it mood 971 against 1158 and sunlight 648 against 1210. The shaft pass is most of what sunlight still costs. A WebGPU validation warning "Draw with a vertex count of 0 is unusual" shows on `?r3d=demo` too (it is not the air's).

**Tests.** In `render3d/air/`: `cracks.test.ts` (the model, the mask on both sides of the rim, the choice and the packing), `crackNodes.test.ts` and `shaftNodes.test.ts` (the shaders build in GLSL and WGSL, with named loops), `shafts.test.ts` (the plan, the sample points, the pixel offset), `air.test.ts`, `dust.test.ts`, `puffs.test.ts`, `drips.test.ts`, `airLight.test.ts` (the simulation with a seeded rng), `airQuads.test.ts`, `airNodes.test.ts`; beside them `airLayer.test.ts` (the pre-pass skips the air meshes), `moodStage.test.ts` (the frame passes the air with smoke from the lit torches, the blast bursts once per crossing, `dust=0` gives no specks, Stir and Draft are shown), `sunlightStage.test.ts` (one crack, two after 2, different rays after R, `vary=0&rays=1&patch=3` gives one ray and `rx = 3 / 2 / 0.81`, `shafts=0`); `game/airmovers.test.ts`, `game/moodDemoScript.test.ts`; and the cases added to `lightNodes.test.ts`, `lights.test.ts`, `sightMaterials.test.ts`, `runView3d.test.ts`, `runStage.test.ts`, `params.test.ts`, `panel.test.ts`, `stats.test.ts`, `quality.test.ts` and `imports.test.ts`.

### Figure models (#338)

Seven creatures as real 3D models, built at run time from a description of their sprite (the four woodland animals are built another way, from tubes: see "Tube models: the woodland animals (#357)" below): the four heroes (wizard, knight for the Paladin, dwarf for the Barbarian, elf for the Elven Ranger), the skeleton (warrior, archer, raised), the ogre and the dire wolf, plus the pack's weapons and a skull. **No run shows them yet:** only the dev pages `?r3d=gallery&focus=models` and `?r3d=woodland&models=1` choose entities for models; every run still draws sprite cards (switching a run is a later issue). Look only: no server or protocol change, but for one fix in shared code (`game/anim/pickFrame.ts`: a figure without take-off, landing or hover frames shows its ground frames instead of throwing, which the gallery's state cycle ran into). The design and the plan are on issue #338; the approved looks are two try-out pages kept with the issue (figures and weapons). Code: `render3d/models/`.

**A creature is a description** (`models/desc.ts`, one file per creature in `models/descs/`, the table `models/table.ts`: `DESCS`, `MODEL_OF_KIND`, `modelKeyOf(kind)`). A `FigureDesc` is a base and a list of add-ons, in sprite pixels (16 columns, rows from the top of a 28 row grid):
- the base: `side` (lines of 16 letters, a side view of head and body with the front on the right, `firstRow` its first sprite row), `colours` (letter to colour), `parts` (head, then body: `rows`, `taper`, `open` for a robe's flat end, `pow` for a squarish ball, an own `side`/`firstRow`, `lie` for a quadruped's body, `paintFrom`), `outline` (the hull's thickness), `centre`, `size` (how big the model is against its card's standing height, 1 = as tall as the card, the default; a round body covers more screen than a flat card, so bulky creatures take less: dwarf 0.77, every other creature but the wizard 0.87, tuned by eye), `metal` (polished steel), `quad` (four legs), `raised` (colours lightened on raised parts), `headAt` and `neck` (where the head sits and turns);
- the add-ons: `eyes`, `visor` (painted lines thinner than a pixel), `hair`, `shells` (beard, hood, cape), `collar`, `seams` (dark unlit rings), `rings` (ribs), `hat`, `spikes` (cones, drops, balls, waves: ears, horns, tusks, a plume, a tail; `sway` and `wag` make them loose), `legs` (two, or two pairs with `at`), `arms` (`metal` for armour).

**The builder** is pure (plain typed arrays, no canvas; it runs under node and in a Web Worker). `grid.ts` reads the side view; `part.ts` turns head and body into stacks of rings (`buildPart`, `vertexNormals`); `paint.ts` stretches the flat colours round each part and sets the eyes and the visor on the front (`paintOf`; `stonePaint`, `skyPaint` for the steel look); `addons.ts` makes the shells, rings, collar, spikes, hat, legs, arms and the outline hulls; `model.ts` (`buildFigure`, `buildSkull`) flattens all of it into one `FigureModel`: vertex streams in the rest pose (sprite px, the front along +x, the feet at y 0), a bone index per vertex, the bones' `pivots` and `parents`, and index ranges per stage in draw order (`STAGES`: legs, body, head, collar, hair, hat, spikes, arms, weapon). `look[3]` of a vertex is 0 lit, 1 steel, 2 unlit (seams). `weapons.ts` builds each pack weapon from its sprite (`WEAPONS` keyed by the sprite name; `buildWeapon(name, px)`): a rounded piece, thick in the middle and thin at the rim, a bow as two rods; the big hammer is redrawn. A weapon model has one bone and its origin at the hand's grip; the grips of the weapons on the figures page are the page's, the others are first guesses by kind.

**A model is as tall as the card it replaces, times its `size`: the game decides sizes, the description only a share** (a description has no `scale`; its optional `size` defaults to 1). `buildFigure(key, desc, height?)` fits `FigureModel.scale` so that the full standing height (feet to the highest vertex of any piece, add-ons and outline hulls included, no weapon) times `scale` equals `height * size`; without a target (tests, node) `scale` is 1, whatever the size (a share of nothing). The model records `FigureModel.size`, so `figures.ts` needs no description lookup. What follows the smaller model on the model path: the weapon (`boneScale` includes `size`: the card's weapon size times `size`), the anchors for bar and name and the opener's mid (the card's numbers times `size`, the name's 3 px gap not scaled), the ground shadow oval (the card's, times `size`; the light's caster is the model's own outline, see "Shadows" below), and everything derived from `scale` (the shoulder height of the swoosh, fangs, swirl). A skull has the fitted skeleton's scale, which includes the skeleton's `size`; a model built without a fit (no target, the skull model) records `size` 1. Unchanged, the card's: the sight `reach`, the swoosh's reach (the damage reach), the root's steps and the card path itself. The kit supplies `height` with the build job (`cardHeight` in `kit.ts`): the opaque height in sprite px of frame 0 of the kind's `FigureDef.idle` (highest opaque row to the foot row, `footY` or the frame's bottom) times `FigureDef.scale`. The kind is `FIT_KIND[key]` in `table.ts`; the skeleton is fitted to the Skeleton Warrior (the archer and the raised skeleton have the same card height, 14 px). An unknown sprite, or a target that is not positive, builds at scale 1. Derived from the model's `scale`: `baseHeight` and `radius`, the hop and lying lift of the root (`root.y`), the shoulder height of the effects and the model's own size on screen. Not from it: the overlay anchors (bar, name, head) and the opener's mid, which are the card path's numbers from the idle frame's bounds, `footY` and `FigureDef.scale` (`standingAnchor` in `figures.ts`, used by both paths; the model path passes the scale times `size`); and the root's steps (lunge, hit jolt, wind-up shiver), which are the game's px times `FigureDef.scale` and the fall shrink, as the card scales its steps. The pose's other dynamic factors are the fall shrink (`1 - fall * 0.2`) and the dash stretch (x 1.25, y 0.9), both in the root's `sx, sy, sz`; the root's stretch also stretches the model's weapon, where the card's weapon is not stretched. Every skull has one size: the fitted skeleton's `scale`, whoever died. A weapon is drawn at the size of the card path (the game's weapon scale times the figure's `FigureDef.scale`): `drawModel` sets the weapon instance's `boneScale` to that divided by the wielder's `scale`.

**The kit** (`kit.ts` `createKit(sprite, runner)`, `build.ts`, `worker.ts`). `kit.get(key)` never throws and never blocks: the first call starts the build in a Web Worker (`workerRunner()`; `directRunner` builds in place for tests) and returns null until the model has arrived, and the caller draws the sprite card meanwhile. Keys: a `DescKey`, `'skull'`, or a weapon's sprite name. The typed arrays are transferred, not copied. A worker that dies fails its pending builds and every later one (a console warning starting "3D model", once per key): those figures stay cards for the rest of the stage. The kit belongs to the stage, not the view: after a lost device the new view finds every model still built. `stageKit.ts` makes the kit of a dev stage (sprite pixels read from the atlas image once) and the readout `window.__r3d.models` (`figures`, `draws`, `builtMs`).

**Skeleton and pose** (`rig.ts`, `pose3d.ts`). 14 bones (`BONES`: hips, body, head, hat, two arms and hands, four legs, two weapon bones; `MAX_BONES`), each a turn about its pivot, a scale and a shift; `composeBones` gives one 3x4 matrix per bone. Up to four bends (`MAX_BENDS`) lean loose pieces (hat tip, plume, ears, tail) and a robe term swings the open hem. `skinVertex` is the CPU reference of the vertex shader and the written spec of `nodes.ts`. `poseFigure(model, def, viewState, dt, mem)` ports the page's `pose`, `attackPose` and the hit: idle, the skip of two legs and the bound of four (`GAIT`), the swing (from the wound-up pose, blended in over `ATTACK_IN_SECS` unless a Windup held it), shoot, cast, bite, slam (fitted to `FigureDef.slam.t`), dash stretch, the hit recoil (`HIT_SECS`), falling, lying, and the held pose of stone and frozen figures. What the game decides stays the game's: attack times, reach, arc, lunge, speed, weapon. A lying (asleep) figure is rolled on its side and rests lower than the ground by `LIE_SINK` (0.3, a first guess) of its side reach, the softness of the body: the root lift is `sideReach * scale * (1 - LIE_SINK) * sy`, the floor's depth hides the part below (the instance's `cut` flag stays for sinking in water), and on ground cover the body rests on the cover's top, sunk by the same amount; the bar anchor's `head` is the visible thickness `(2 - LIE_SINK) * sideReach * scale`. The returned `Pose3d` is owned by `mem` (written in place each call; `copyPose` to keep one). No knees, elbows or jaw.

**Drawing** (`instances.ts`, `nodes.ts`, `layer.ts`). `Figures3d` (`figures.ts`) draws an entity as a model when the stage chose it (`FigureLook.models = { kit, is(e) }`), its kind has a description and the model is built; otherwise as a card. A model figure pushes `FigureInst`s into `FigureOut.models`: the body, its weapon (a one-bone model on the wielder's `weaponR`, a bow on `weaponL`, at the wielder's pivot), dash afterimages, the fading copy of a figure that vanished, and skulls for the dead. Each instance becomes one row of a float RGBA data texture (`ROW_TEXELS` 64 texels: root 3, bones 14 x 3, bends 4, robe, tint and stone, alpha and sight and the caster slot, flags, and since #359 `ROW.extra`, one texel of flare and eye for the spectres' painted eyes, flare 0 and eye 1 for every other row; `packInstance`); the vertex stage reads its row with `textureLoad` and skins the vertex (bend or robe, bone, root, then the camera's height stretch `1 / cos(pitch)` that cards have too). The texture grows by rows (`growRows`). Seven vertex attributes, under the limit of 8 buffers.
- **Outline: an inverted hull, cut by an id pass.** The hull is the model seen from inside (back faces pushed out along the normal, flat dark). Per pixel two rules decide what shows of it: (1) a hull fragment passes the depth test against everything opaque, the pieces of all figures included; (2) it is cut where the nearest piece is a piece of its own figure with a stage at or above its own (`STAGES`). So an arm's outline shows over the body (arms come later), the body's outline never shows over an arm, and two legs have no line between them. Who the nearest piece is, rule 2 reads from the id pass.
- **The id pass and the three draws.** Before the picture the view draws the pieces of every model figure into the id target (`idTarget` in `view.ts`: RGBA8, its own depth buffer, cleared to 0, nearest filtering). A texel holds the owner number in two bytes and the stage + 1 in the third (0: no figure). The id material has the piece's vertex stage and its stipple (sight, alpha, `cut`), so the target matches the picture's piece pixels; it draws no world, so the id fragment is kept only at or above a height per figure, the fourth float of the row's flags (`FigureInst.groundY`, from `hidingHeightAt` in `dips/shape.ts`: 0 on the floor, `LAVA_LEVEL` in lava, `NOTHING_HIDDEN` where the figure wades or stands over a chasm). Without it a piece below the floor would cover the hull and cut the outline at the feet. The pass runs once per frame, only when a model figure is drawn (`layer.drawn.figures > 0`); a run that shows cards only pays nothing. The picture then draws, per model, **one piece draw** (`pieceIndex`, `FIGURE_ORDER`) and **one hull draw** (`lineIndex`, `HULL_ORDER` = `FIGURE_ORDER + 0.5`, after the pieces of every model), however many figures, overlaps and stages: the count grows with the number of models only (`layer.drawn.draws` counts id, piece and hull draws). The hull reads the id target with a texel fetch at its own pixel (`textureLoad` at `screenCoordinate`) and is cut by rule 2. It writes depth, so whatever is drawn after it (water, effects, air) is cut by the outline.
- **Size rule of the id target.** The id target always has exactly the size of the target the picture is drawn into (`view.ts` sets it before the pass: the plan's target, or the drawing buffer when High draws straight to the canvas). On WebGL2 three flips both `screenCoordinate` and the texel fetch of a render-target texture by the height of the bound framebuffer and of the texture; the two cancel only when the sizes are equal. `readback()` uses the id target only if its size equals its own redraw's, otherwise it draws the hulls as plain ones.
- **Owner and stage per vertex.** The owner number is the third float of the row's flags (`OWNER_MAX` 65536, modulo): the index of the instance's owner (the entity) among the frame's owners in order of first appearance, so two figures never share one; a weapon and a dash copy carry their owner's number, and each skull has an owner of its own (a negative number no entity has). The stage is in `skin.y` of the layer's GPU copy of the model (`stageSkin(m)`, from `FigureModel.ranges`; ranges share no vertices); the model's own `skin[1]` stays the hull flag that `model.ts` and `pose3d.ts` read.
- **Other cameras: `idOn`.** `makeFigureShared()` gives `{ cosP, idOn }`, uniforms every figure material of a view shares. The view sets `idOn` to 1 while the id target holds this frame's pass (the picture) and 0 for the water's mirror camera and the shafts' depth camera, which run before it and have no id pass. There the hull is a plain depth-tested hull, so a reflection can show a line where a figure's body passes in front of its own arm. A frame without model figures runs no id pass.
- **Materials** (`figureMaterials`, `FigureMaterials = { piece, line, id, sun }`): the piece (painted from one paint texture per model; lit), the hull (`line`), the id and the sun's depth pass. Per model one mesh each: id (in the id scene), piece, hull, and the sun's (not counted in `draws`), all instanced; the layer's geometries share the model's vertex streams and only the index list and the `row` attribute differ.
- **Light.** A piece is lit at its true point and normal through the view's `lightAt`, with a wrap of `1 - FIGURE_SHADE` (0.55, a first guess as on the page) so the side away from a lamp is not black. Steel vertices read the sky paint by the reflected view direction. The hull and the unlit seams take the ambient light only, so a figure in the dark has no grey outline; under a lamp a model's outline is therefore darker than a card's dark pixels.
- **Shadows.** In the woodland the sun's pass draws the true mesh into the shadow map (a figure the hero cannot see, or a faint one under alpha 0.5, casts none). Against point lights **every** model casts its own outline: the model seen from the side in its rest pose, baked into the atlas by `npm run atlas` as the frame `model_<key>` (`outlineFrame(key)` in `table.ts`; `buildModelOutlines` in `scripts/model-frames.ts`, below). `drawModel` hands `casterOf` that frame at the model's `scale * variation size * pose scale`, mirrored with the facing, so the light shader still reads one atlas rect per caster and the per-frame cost is that of a card. The shadow does not follow the pose (a swing or a stride changes nothing in it). A lying, faint (alpha below 1) or falling figure casts none. The soft ground oval stays.
- **Sight.** `figureSight` (`sightAttrs.ts`) fills each instance's `sight` in the view before the layer packs it, with the reach the figure's card would have, so a model is seen and hidden exactly where its card would be; `hiddenByPicture` counts model owners.
- **States.** Tint (poison, slowed, chilled, tainted, the dark of falling and drowning, a wind-up's own tint; the hurt flash is not a tint on a model, the hit shows as the recoil), stone (grey paint), frozen (tint, held pose), alpha (invisible, fading), `cut` (nothing below the ground while sinking or drowning), `dark` (a remembered figure). A lying figure rests on the ground cover.

**The effects pass** (`models/fx.ts`, `fxMaterial` in `materials.ts`, one `DynMesh` in `view.ts`). Flat-coloured triangles in an `FxBuf` (`position`, `rgba`, `n`): vertex colours, blended, no depth write, `renderOrder` 5, on the air pre-pass's skip layer. Three shapes fill it, on the model path only (`Figures3d.smallEffects`; cards get none, and stone, frozen, dark and lying figures show none): `swooshTris` (the fading arc of a melee swing at shoulder height, `reach` and `arc` from `FigureDef.swing`, in the swing's colour where the page is white; it fades through the return), `fangTris` (two rows of five teeth and their glow facing the camera, in front of a biter's muzzle; open in the wind-up, clamping with the bite; lifted along the view ray so the head does not hide them) and `swirlTris` (three arcs circling a channelling figure, in `FigureDef.base`; not on the page). The anchor heights take the same `1 / cos(pitch)` stretch as the figure; the shapes do not. A sleeper's "Z" is the overlay's (`overlayLayout`), from the anchor's effects. **#308 takes this pass over** for the game's effects: `FxBuf` and `fxMaterial` are its start.

**Dev pages.** `?r3d=gallery&focus=models`: a row per model kind cycling through the states, the effect rows (frozen, stone, poisoned, burning, sinking and so on) and a row that dies and leaves skulls. Options: `&state=`, `&slow=`, `&t=`, `&aim=<deg>` (90 faces the camera), `&at=x,z` or `&at=model:<i>` / `effect:<row>,<col>` / `dying:<i>` / `crowd:<i>`, `&at=variety:<row>[,<col>]` (the variety block of the animals, #357), `&focus=lineup` (the **size line-up**, #356: every distinct model key once, 24 of them since #359, shortest left, on one line at equal depth under the work light, idle and facing right, behind a wall row so no other block shows above them; the order is `LINEUP_KEYS` in `galleryStage.ts` and a test builds the models and checks it against `baseHeight`; `LINEUP_PITCH` 2.0 tiles, ids `ID.lineup` 4700 `+ place`; the whole row is in one picture at `&zoom=1`, closer in halves with `&at=lineup:<place>` at `&zoom=3`: `lineup:4` and `lineup:14`), `&at=spectre:<i>` (the spectre block of #359, places 0 to 5; see "Ghost and Shade (#359)"), `&crowd=<n>` (walking model figures; `&crowdAs=cards` draws the same crowd as cards, for the cost; `&crowdKinds=<model key>,...` lets the crowd wear only those models, e.g. `stag,deer,boar,bunny`), `&light=torch` (the pillars' torches instead of the work light). `?r3d=woodland&models=1`: seven model figures under the sun. `?r3d=liquids&models=<stag|deer|boar|bunny|wolf>`: that kind in every role of the liquids page (#357). `tools/e2e/models-cost.mjs <baseUrl> <label>` measures the crowd's frame time as models against cards on both backends and both lights (`GPU`, `SECS`, `CROWD`, `KINDS`; it fails on a software renderer, a missing WebGPU adapter, a console error or a lost device), and `tools/e2e/shots-diff.py <dirA> <dirB> <outDir>` compares two shot folders (differing pixels per picture, magenta over B). `tools/e2e/models-shots.mjs` takes the check shots of both (every kind in every state, the effect block, the torch light, the skulls, the crowd both ways, two hours in the woodland, the spectre block, a lost device, a lost device with a Ghost and with a Shade in view) and fails on any console error, model build warning or lost device. `?r3d=gallery&focus=lineup&circles=1` draws the server's hit circle of every line-up figure as a yellow ring on the ground (`lineupCircles` in `galleryStage.ts`: `HITBOX[kind].r`, a hero's `PLAYER_RADIUS`; the ring's outer edge is the circle), to judge a circle against its body.

**Adding a creature.** Write `models/descs/<name>.ts` (copy the nearest one; the side view is the sprite's idle frame seen from the side), add it to `DESCS` and its kinds to `MODEL_OF_KIND` in `table.ts`, look at it in the gallery (`&aim=90` for the paint) and add it to `descs.test.ts`. A new weapon needs a `WEAPONS` entry with its hand grip. A creature that is not a head and a body on a card (a neck, bending legs, antlers) is a tube creature: see "Adding a tube creature" below. Either way, run `npm run atlas` so the lamp outline `model_<key>` exists, and add the creature to `MODEL_OF_KIND`.

**Cost** (#338, #364). Dev notebook, RTX 3060 (WebGL2 through ANGLE over Vulkan and WebGPU), the `gpu.mjs` start line with `GPU=uncapped`, a 2560 x 1280 window, production build, 10 s each (`tools/e2e/models-cost.mjs`, measured 2026-11-18). `?r3d=gallery&focus=models` with `&crowd=40`: the 40 walking figures as models against the same 40 as cards (`&crowdAs=cards`); both draw the model block (195 instances) besides, so 260 and 195 figures as models. The numbers are the **median of the three runs' median frame times** (the mean is useless here: every run has a stall of about a second, p99 near 950 ms, which comes from the machine and not from the page). The old code and the new were measured in the same sitting, alternately, from two production builds (commit `66a381f` and this one). The gate: models minus cards at most 3 ms per row.

| | old: models / cards | old: models - cards | new: models / cards | new: models - cards | figure draws, old / new |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| WebGL2, work light | 12.0 / 10.9 ms | +1.1 ms | 5.5 / 6.1 ms | -0.6 ms | 282 / 54 |
| WebGL2, `&light=torch` | 16.4 / 10.5 ms | +5.9 ms | 5.8 / 5.8 ms | 0.0 ms | 282 / 54 |
| WebGPU, work light | 8.5 / 7.2 ms | +1.3 ms | 4.9 / 4.9 ms | 0.0 ms | 306 / 54 |
| WebGPU, `&light=torch` | 8.6 / 7.4 ms | +1.2 ms | 4.9 / 4.8 ms | +0.1 ms | 306 / 54 |

The same with the four woodland animals as the crowd (`&crowdKinds=stag,deer,boar,bunny`; 235 figures as models, 195 as cards):

| | old: models / cards | old: models - cards | new: models / cards | new: models - cards | figure draws, old / new |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| WebGL2, work light | 12.0 / 14.0 ms | -2.0 ms | 5.7 / 5.8 ms | -0.1 ms | 268 / 54 |
| WebGL2, `&light=torch` | 10.7 / 10.6 ms | +0.1 ms | 6.0 / 5.9 ms | +0.1 ms | 257 / 54 |
| WebGPU, work light | 7.6 / 7.3 ms | +0.3 ms | 4.7 / 4.6 ms | +0.1 ms | 257 / 54 |
| WebGPU, `&light=torch` | 7.7 / 7.2 ms | +0.5 ms | 4.8 / 5.0 ms | -0.2 ms | 257 / 54 |

Every row is under the 3 ms gate; the new difference is within the noise (the three runs of a row differ by 0.1 to 0.5 ms on WebGPU, and by up to 6.6 ms on WebGL2 in the tube crowd (cards, work light: 5.8 to 12.4), where one run had a slower stretch). The old WebGL2 rows vary more (the torch row came out between 12.8 and 26.2 ms in its models runs); their medians are the middle of three. The whole page is also faster than before (about 5 ms against 7 to 16), because the model block's own draws shrank too. The figure draws are **the same for 260 figures and for 493** (`&crowd=184`): 54 (the old code made 325 and 474 in the check shots). A frame's draws are an id, a piece and a hull draw per model on screen, not per figure, batch or stage; the 54 are those of the models the page shows. Build times in the worker, once per model: wizard 90 to 150 ms, knight 145 to 200 ms, dwarf 55 to 75 ms, elf 55 to 75 ms, skeleton 35 to 45 ms, ogre 60 to 75 ms, wolf 55 to 60 ms, a weapon 3 to 45 ms, the skull 50 to 60 ms; none of it on the frame.

**Tests.** In `render3d/models/`: `grid.test.ts`, `part.test.ts`, `paint.test.ts`, `addons.test.ts`, `model.test.ts` and `descs.test.ts` (every description builds; bones, stages, normals, the unlit seams, the bend limit), `weapons.test.ts` (every pack weapon, the hand grips), `kit.test.ts` (null until built, one build per key, a dead worker), `rig.test.ts` (`composeBones`, `skinVertex`), `pose3d.test.ts` (gaits, attacks and their timing, the held pose, no allocation of the returned pose), `instances.test.ts` (the row layout, the owner number and `groundY`, `growRows`), `nodes.test.ts` (the four materials build in GLSL and WGSL; the hull reads the id texel at its own pixel and the piece does not; the id pass's height cut; the outline and the seams through the ambient), `layer.test.ts` (`stageSkin`; per model an id, a piece and a hull draw on the right index lists, every hull after every piece, one owner number per owner, the sun's draws), `fx.test.ts`; beside them the cases added to `figures.test.ts` (the model path, weapons, skulls, the small effects), `sightAttrs.test.ts`, `sightMaterials.test.ts`, `galleryStage.test.ts`, `woodlandStage.test.ts` and `stats.test.ts`. The tube animals' own tests are listed under "Tube models".

### Tube models: the woodland animals (#357)

Stag, deer, boar and bunny (`KIND.Stag`, `Deer`, `Boar`, `Bunny` in `MODEL_OF_KIND`) are 3D models that are **not** built from their sprites. A 16 px side view wrapped round a head and a body (the #338 way) cannot make a neck, long bending legs or antlers, so a creature has a second way of being shaped. **Two shape sources sit behind one `FigureModel`** (`models/model.ts`): a `FigureDesc` (`desc.ts`, built by `buildFigure`: the heroes, skeleton, ogre, wolf) or a `TubeDesc` (`tubeDesc.ts`, built by `buildTubeFigure` in `tubeModel.ts`: the four animals). Everything downstream is the same: the kit and its worker, instancing, hulls, the id pass, light, sight, the sun's shadow pass, `composeBones` and `skinVertex`. `build.ts` picks by key (`isTubeKey`), `table.ts` holds both tables (`DESCS`, `TUBE_DESCS`; the key types `DescKey`, `TubeKey` and `ModelKey` = both). Use a `FigureDesc` for a creature that is a head and a body with arms or two legs and should follow its card; use a `TubeDesc` for one that needs a neck, bending legs, antlers or tusks, or that starts new and need only be recognisable. Still no run shows them (#355); the cards of the animals remain what a run draws.

**The shapes** (`models/tubes.ts`, pure, typed arrays, `three/webgpu` maths only; the model's px are the unit). `tube(points, opts)` is a round tube along a line of points `{ p, r, wide }` (radius, and `wide` flattens a ring sideways), rounded at both ends; options `K` (points round a ring), `N` (rings along), `cap`, `pow` (towards a square ring) and `ref` (the direction the ring's first axis is taken against). `ball(rx, ry, rz, pow?, turnZ?)` is an ellipsoid. `drop(r, len, sink, wide, dir)` is a rounded point (ears, tails, tusks, tines): pointed pieces are drops, never cones, because a cone's hull makes slivers. Tube radii below about 0.35 px vanish at game size. All three return the `Mesh3` of `part.ts` (position, normal, uv, index).

**The description** (`TubeDesc`: `name`, `size`, `outline`, `pieces`, `pivots`, `parents`, `legs`, `footprint`, optional `hop`). A `TubePiece` is a `shape`, an optional `at` (where its origin sits), a `stage` (one of `STAGES`), a `bone`, a `colour`, a hull `line` (thickness in page px at size 1, 0 for none, as for the eyes), an optional `turn` (about z, before it is placed: how a hind leg's rest angle is built into the geometry, so the identity pose is the standing animal), an optional `bend` and `torso`. Units are the try-out page's px, where one page px is one wolf-model px. **Pieces that share a stage share one outline**: the body and the neck are two tubes in the `body` stage, and because hulls are drawn first without depth no line shows where the neck leaves the chest. The layering the looks rely on: legs, then body and neck, then head, then muzzle, ears and tail (`hair`), then nose, eyes, antlers and tusks (`spikes`). **Colour:** a flat colour per piece, except one *painted* piece (the torso): its `colour` is a function `(u, d)` of how far along the tube (0 at the rump) and how far round from the top (0 top, 1 underside), evaluated into `FigureModel.paint` (128 x 64; u along, v round), which gives the coat, the pale belly and the white rump. The coats are set by region, not copied from sprite pixels; every kind has one coat (the red-brown set), because a tint can lighten or darken a coat but never turn it into another. One painted piece at most per description (`check` throws otherwise, as it does for more than `MAX_BENDS` bending pieces and for a parent that comes after its child in `BONES`; the messages name the description). Eyes are lit balls without a hull.

**Bone use of a four-legged tube model.** Each model carries its own `parents`, so the shader and `composeBones` stay unchanged. Front legs: the arm bones are the upper legs (`armR`, `armL`) and the hand bones the lower legs (`handR`, `handL`, chained to their arm). Hind legs: `leg0`/`leg1` and `leg2`/`leg3` as two chains. `head` is a child of `body`; `hat` is a child of `head` and carries the antlers (the stag's; the deer's build has none, so its `hat` bone is unused and it does not butt) or the tusks (boar). A model without lower leg pieces (the bunny) leaves `lower` out of `legs` and squashes the upper leg instead. **Bends** (at most `MAX_BENDS` 4): the neck bends as a whole (`bend: { neck: { origin, from, to } }`, weights rising smoothly with `smooth` from `from` to `to` along the neck tube's u, so the chest does not move; the pose moves and turns the head bone to stay on the neck's end), and loose ears and tails use the game's existing bend driver (`bend: { sway, wag }`: ears `sway` 0.8, tails `sway` 1 and `wag` 1, as the wolf's). Their direction and length come from the shape (a drop's own, or a tube's first point to its last). Stag and deer use all four (neck, tail, two ears); boar three; bunny two (its tail is a fixed ball).

**Model data and the pose abilities it switches on.** `poseFigure` (`pose3d.ts`) stays the one pose function; nothing in it, in `figures.ts` or the draw code is keyed on a name or a kind. These optional `FigureModel` fields, set by `buildTubeFigure`, turn abilities on (`crest` is not one: it is `Pose3dMemory.crest`, which `drawModel` sets from the variation):

| Field | Set from | What `poseFigure` does with it |
|---|---|---|
| `legs` | `TubeDesc.legs` (`upper`, `lower`, `front`, bone indices) | the leg chains: the upper leg swings (`GAIT`'s bound, the same for every four-legged model, so the wolf does not change); a lower piece folds back while its leg swings forward (`LEG_FOLD` 0.95 rad); without a lower piece the upper squashes as before. A model without `legs` gets the four leg bones as pairs (memoised per model). |
| `hop` | `TubeDesc.hop` | multiplies the bound's height (the bunny's 1.3; 1 when absent). |
| `neckBend` | the index in `bends` of the neck, -1 for a model with none | the neck bends against the body's pitch with a nod in the gait, an idle look-around and a lowered neck in the butt; the head is moved and turned to stay on the neck's end and held level against the rock. A neckless model only tilts its head. |
| `butt` | some piece sits on the `hat` bone | `styleOf` gives an armless model the attack style `butt` instead of `bite`: rear back (`BUTT.back` 0.35 of the strike), lunge (the game's `lunge`; none for the stag, which has none) with the neck bent down, or, without a neck, the head thrown up (boar), and back. A wind-up holds the reared pose (`BUTT.hold` 0.35); the strike and the return last `BUTT.secs` 0.36 s. A `butt` shows no fangs, in the wind-up or the strike (the fang rule in `drawModel`: fangs for an armless model that does not butt and whose kind has a melee swing, `FigureDef.swing`: the wolf; the deer and the bunny have none and show no attack); the swoosh at the damage reach from `FigureDef.swing` stays. |
| `crest` | the instance's variation (`Pose3dMemory.crest`, default 1) | scales the `hat` bone (antlers, tusks) on all three axes. A stone or frozen figure keeps the first value in its held pose. |

Every state the game has works for these models: wind-up, hit recoil, fall, lying asleep, stone and frozen, sinking with `cut`, the dash stretch. `LEG_FOLD` and `BUTT` are exported from `pose3d.ts`. All these numbers are first guesses; the user judges the motion in the gallery.

**Size: the model states its own height.** A tube model is not fitted to its card, because it does not resemble it. Its `scale` is `desc.size * PAGE_TO_GAME` (`tubeModel.ts`, 1.0856: the Dire Wolf model's fitted `scale`, so the page's ratio to the wolf holds in the game); its `size` field is 1. It records `stands` (the highest vertex of any piece, hulls not counted, in model px) and `footprint` (half length and half width of the ground oval, from the description). On the model path `stands` replaces the card for what the card used to say: the anchors for the bar and the name (`stands * scale * variation size * FigureDef.scale`, the name 3 px above it), the opener's mid and the standing height; `footprint` replaces `FigureDef.baseR` for the ground shadow oval (long for a four-legged body, turned by the facing) and the sight reach (the half length). Standing heights in game px: stag about 23.3, deer 15.8, boar 11.1, bunny 8.1, against the Dire Wolf's 16.5.

**Variation** (`models/variation.ts`). `variationOf(id)` returns `{ size, tone, crest }` from a hash of the entity id (a 32-bit murmur-style finaliser with a salt per number), so it is the same on every client without server data, stable per call, and inside the limits for any integer id (negative ones too). Limits `VARY`: size 0.94 to 1.06 (the body instance's `scale`, so everything derived from the size follows: the anchors, the footprint oval, the sight reach, the lamp outline's caster scale, the swoosh's shoulder height, the lying figure's head cap and a weapon's scale), tone 0.9 to 1.1 (multiplies the instance tint after `modelTint`, so a poison or frost tint is kept and a stone animal stays grey; the white rump and the antlers are toned too, which is right for a lighter or darker animal), crest 0.8 to 1.2 (the `crest` above). Only a model with `varies` (every tube model) uses it. Shape variants (other legs, ears or antler branches per animal) are not built. The limits are first values for the user's eye; the variety rows of the gallery show them.

**The server's circle.** One circle per creature (`radius` in `server/src/defs/enemies.rs`). For every tube model (the four animals and the nine two-legged creatures) it is `hitRadius(desc)` (`tubeModel.ts`): the mean of the torso tube's half length and half width in game px (the widest radius times its flattening, no hull, no neck, head or legs), rounded, not below 4 so a small creature is not harder to hit (#323). Results (#356 Task 12, set after the user approved the looks and sizes): stag 5; deer, boar and bunny 4; Goblin Archer, both orcs, Necromancer, Summoner, Disciple, Imp, Chort and Knight 4 (before: deer 5, boar 6, orcs 6, the others 5, the goblin 4). Every upright torso is slimmer than the floor of 4, so the nine share one circle; `Deer | Stag` is one server block with two radii. `FIGURES[kind].baseR` (`game/anim/defs.ts`, the predicted melee check, the ground shadow of a card) holds the same numbers: `tubeModel.test.ts` lists the expected `hitRadius` of every key and fails where a kind's `baseR` differs from its model's; the server's numbers are pinned in the stat-block tests of `enemies.rs` and exported as `HITBOX`. **A monster's melee range is its weapon's reach plus its body** (`sheet.rs`), so the smaller circles shortened the Strike ranges too (Orc Warrior 26 to 24, Orc Archer 22 to 20, Necromancer, Summoner, Disciple and Imp 21 to 20, Chort and Knight 23 to 22, Boar 24 to 22; `enemy_kits_are_pinned`), and the orc's swoosh (`FIGURES`, `swing.reach` 24) with them. `plan_3a_fight_is_pinned` was re-recorded once for it (the wizard's fight against an Orc Warrior). The tuning numbers are `balancing`.

**The lamp outline** is baked, not drawn (see "Shadows" above). `buildModelOutlines(width)` in `client/scripts/model-frames.ts` builds every model of `DESCS` and `TUBE_DESCS` (the frame is drawn from the model's positions in model px; the model's `scale` does not enter), drops z, and fills a pixel when its centre lies in a triangle or a vertex lies in it; the frame is `2 * ceil(max |x|)` wide and `ceil(max y)` tall, centred on x 0 with the feet on the bottom row, opaque in the outline colour 0x222222 (the shader reads only alpha). `build-atlas.ts` appends the strip last; the frame names are `model_<key>` (`outlineFrame`). A new model, or a changed shape, needs `npm run atlas` (the atlas is regenerated, not committed). The atlas was 512 × 1982 px before #356 and is 512 × 1984 px with the eight more outline frames of #356 (the strip is one row; see "Two-legged tube figures"), 64 px under the 2048 px that WebGL2 guarantees as a minimum texture size; `build-atlas.ts` fails above 2048 px. A dev page that builds its own frame map must take these rects from `atlas.json`, or no lamp shadow is cast, silently.

**Dev pages.** `?r3d=gallery&focus=models` shows the four animals in the model rows (every state, the effect rows `KIND.Stag` and `KIND.Boar` among them) and a **variety block** below the crowd: one row per kind (since #356 eight: stag, deer, boar, bunny, goblin archer, orc warrior, imp, chort), six figures each with consecutive ids (`ID.variety` 4500, id `+ row * 10 + col`), so the variation is seen side by side; `&at=variety:<row>` puts the camera over the middle of a row, `&at=variety:<row>,<col>` over one animal (`VARIETY_ROWS`, `VARIETY_COLS`, `VARIETY_PITCH` in `galleryMap.ts`). The model kinds (24 since #359: the Ghost and the Shade) stand in rows of `MODEL_PER_ROW` 11, so in three rows, the rows `MODEL_KIND_ROW_PITCH` 7 tiles apart (the dying row wraps the same way); each row has four pillar torches (`PILLAR_X` 3, 12, 21, 30), so `&light=torch` reaches every kind from a torch of its own row. The effect block has 14 columns (`MODEL_EFFECT_COLS`; ids `+ row * MODEL_EFFECT_STRIDE` 20 `+ col`): the four heroes, a skeleton, the ogre, the wolf, the stag, the boar, the Necromancer, the Imp, the Knight, the Ghost and the Shade, so `&at=effect:<row>,10` shows the three kinds of #356 side by side and column 12 and 13 are the two spectres. `?r3d=liquids&models=<key>` takes every key of `MODEL_OF_KIND` (`modelKindOf` in `liquidsStage.ts`; e.g. `stag`, `deer`, `boar`, `bunny`, `wolf`, `skelet`, `ogre`, `goblin`, `orc`, `necromancer`, `summoner`, `disciple`, `imp`, `chort`, `npcKnight`; a key worn by several kinds gives the first of them, so `orc` is the Orc Warrior) and makes that kind play every role of the page (the wader, the lava walker, both pushed figures) as a 3D model, with the kit made only when the option is given (`LiquidsOpts.models`, `window.__r3d.models`; `liquidsScript(t, dt, pushes, as?)` takes the kind). `tools/e2e/models-shots.mjs` covers the animals in every state, an aimed shot per animal, the variety rows (eight), the torch light on the stag and three liquids shots per animal (wade, chasm, lava). For the nine two-legged kinds (#356) it adds an aimed shot in the attack state (`aim-<kind>`), a torch-light shot, and the three liquids shots each, and the effect shots (`effects-asleep`, `-top` (stone), `-frozen`, `-middle`, `-bottom`) look at column 10.

**Adding a tube creature.**
1. Write `models/descs/<name>.ts` exporting a `TubeDesc` (copy `boar.ts` for a creature without a neck, `deer.ts` for one with; for a two-legged figure see "Adding a two-legged creature" in "Two-legged tube figures (#356)" below): the pieces in page px, the pivots and parents of the bones it uses, the `legs` list, the `footprint`, a `hop` if it should bound higher. Keep tubes at 0.35 px or more, pointed pieces as drops, one painted torso marked `torso: true`.
2. Register it in `table.ts`: add the name to `TubeKey`, the description to `TUBE_DESCS`, and the game kinds to `MODEL_OF_KIND`.
3. Add it to the per-key cases of `tubeModel.test.ts` and `descs.test.ts` (it builds; bones, parents and bends within limits; finite numbers; the pose in every `ANIM`).
4. Set the circle: compute `hitRadius(TUBE_DESCS[key])` and put it in `radius` in `server/src/defs/enemies.rs` and in `baseR` of `FIGURES[kind]` in `game/anim/defs.ts` (the test in `tubeModel.test.ts` lists the expected values per kind; update the server's own stat-block test too).
5. Run `npm run atlas` in `client/` so the lamp outline `model_<key>` exists; the lamp shadow of a model without one is missing silently.
6. Look at it on `?r3d=gallery&focus=models` (every state, `&aim=90`, `&at=variety:<row>` after adding it to `VARIETY_KINDS` in `galleryStage.ts` if the variation should be judged) and, if it wades or burns, on `?r3d=liquids&models=<key>` (add the key to the options there).

**Sizes against the human (#356 rework 1, part G).** A human is `HUMAN_HEIGHT` 18 px (1.75 m). The animals' sizes come from `ANIMAL_FACTOR` (`tubeDesc.ts`, `fitSize`): the deer's shoulder (the chest's top) 9 px (size 0.88 to 0.80), the boar's 8 px (0.9 to 0.70), the sitting bunny's ears' base 3.5 px (0.75 to 0.51); the stag keeps 0.95 (shoulder 11.6, head top 16.1). Built heights (`baseHeight` / with antlers or ears): stag 16.1 / 23.6, deer 12.4 / 13.6, boar 6.0 / 8.5, bunny 3.7 / 5.5. The server's hit circles follow these sizes since Task 12 (5, 4, 4, 4; see "The server's circle" above).

**Tests.** `models/tubes.test.ts` (the shapes), `tubeModel.test.ts` (every description builds; bones, parents, bends and colours; finite positions, normals, pivots and bends; the stag's antler thickness; `hitRadius`, and the stag's `baseR` matching it), `variation.test.ts` (stable per id, inside `VARY` for odd ids), the pose cases of `pose3d.test.ts` (the wolf's table pinned to 1e-6 so the shared gait does not move, the leg chains, hop, neck, butt, crest, finite results for the four animals in every `ANIM` at `dt` 0 and 1/60, held stone and frozen poses), the model-path cases of `figures.test.ts` (anchors from `stands`, oval and reach from `footprint`, no fangs for a butt, the caster from the outline frame) and `scripts/model-frames.test.ts` (the outline frames). The gallery's and the liquids page's tests cover the variety block, `&at=variety:`, the effect rows, `parseLiquids` and `liquidsScript`.

### Two-legged tube figures (#356)

Nine more creatures are 3D models, built from tubes like the animals but standing and walking on two legs: the Goblin Archer (`goblin`), the Orc Warrior and Orc Archer (both `orc`: one model, the archer is the same orc holding its kind's bow, `FigureDef.weapon`; the user, rework 1), the Necromancer, Summoner and Disciple (`necromancer`, `summoner`, `disciple`), the Imp (`imp`), the Chort (`chort`) and the Knight NPC (`npcKnight`). The same rules as for the animals hold: look only, **no run shows them yet** (only the dev pages), no protocol change, and the server's circle is `hitRadius(desc)`, 4 for all nine (set in Task 12 after the user approved the looks; see "The server's circle" above). The design and the plan are on issue #356.

**What the builder gained** (`tubeDesc.ts`, `tubeModel.ts`, `model.ts`). `TubeDesc.stance: 'two'` marks an upright figure; `arms` (`length`, `radius`, `stretch`) makes two arms that hang from the pivots of `armR` and `armL`, each ending in a hand ball; a model without `arms` has none and bites (`styleOf`), so a caster must have them (the Disciple has sleeve arms with no hands shown). `stretch` is the share of a blow's arm lengthening the arms take (`FigureModel.armStretch`, default 1; the Imp uses 0.3, because its arms are of a natural length; arms with an elbow are never lengthened). `arms.elbow` (the upper arm's length; `FigureModel.elbow`) gives the arms an elbow, see "Holding weapons" below. A weapon's grip (the pivots of `weaponR` / `weaponL`) is the middle of the hand ball, `arms.length` below the shoulder (`FigureModel.handY`); before rework 1 it lay 0.8 arm radii higher, in the wrist. `weapon` scales the held weapon (`FigureModel.weaponScale`; the robed three use 0.8). `robeTop` and a piece's `robe: true` make the part of a piece below that height swing as a hem. A piece's `metal: true` gives it the steel look. A piece's `glow: true` makes it glow (look value 3, `nodes.ts`: the piece shows its own colour times the figure's tint whatever light falls on it, in the dark too; a stone figure's does not glow; the Imp's and the Chort's eyes, colour `DEMON_EYE` in `tubeDesc.ts`). A bend's `flutter: { rate, angle }` adds a sway at `rate` Hz to the tip, also standing; without more it turns about the line of sight (x), the two sides opposite ways. With `flutter.axis` (a unit vector in the rest pose) the beat turns about that axis: a wing names a line in its own surface across its span, so it beats across its surface (`pose3d.ts` adds `axis * angle * sin` to the bend's turn; no creature is named there). A bend's `hinge` (0 to 1, default 1) is the share of the piece's length over which the bend builds up: below 1 the rest of the piece is stiff and turns about the foot like a hinged wing. `bend: { with: piece }` makes a piece bend as part of an earlier swaying piece (the same foot, line and turn; it takes none of the four bends): a wing's bones and claw on its membrane. A leg's `knee: -1` bends the other way (a hock). The builder throws, naming the description and the bone, for a piece on a bone that has no pivot: it would turn about the origin.

**The walk** (`pose3d.ts`, `WALK = { swing: 0.5, knee: 0.9 }`, `FigureModel.gait: 'walk'`, which every two-legged model sets). The upper legs swing 0.5 rad in turn, the knee of the leg coming forward bends back by up to 0.9 rad (the other way for `knee: -1`), the body dips as the legs spread and does not hop, and the arms do not lift sideways. A model with no legs (the robes) glides: it leans and rocks, and the hem swings. A sleeping figure lies with straight knees, an entangled one stands with straight legs; a stone or frozen one keeps the pose of the frame it began in, mid-stride included. The four-legged and the #338 figures keep their gait; `models/heroTable.ts` is a recorded pose table of the #338 figures, used only by tests to prove it.

**The tint rule.** A model never wears `FigureDef.tint` (`untinted(d)` in `figures.ts`; the card's tint belongs to its sprite): the Disciple's red tint is not worn by its model, which is painted in its own robe colour (0x531d28), and a summoned Imp, a raised skeleton and every other minion are never tinted. The poison, slowed and frost tints are kept. In a melee the model path draws no weapon at the moment of the blow (as on the Paladin); the swoosh shows the reach. This is the card's rule, not a gap: `weaponPose` in `game/anim/pose.ts` fades a swung blade out in the first 0.2 of `MELEE_T` and in again after 0.7 (the standing decision "a swoosh at the real damage reach replaces the visible full swing"), and `drawModel` in `figures.ts` takes the weapon's `alpha` from that pose. A wind-up shows the weapon.

**Holding weapons** (rework 1, `pose3d.ts`: `ELBOW`, `CARRY`, `elbowArms`, `drawArm`). A straight arm that hangs at the side cannot hold an upright weapon: the weapon stands inside the arm. So the weapon carriers (goblin, orc, Knight and the three robes) have **arms with an elbow**: the description's `arms.elbow` puts the hand bones' pivots at the elbow, the pieces of the upper arm hang on `armR` / `armL`, the forearm's, the cuff's and the fist's on `handR` / `handL` (the bones are the same 14; the weapon bones stay children of the hand bones). `poseFigure` reads only `FigureModel.elbow` and the kind's `FigureDef` (which hand carries: `weaponBoneOf(d.weapon)`): no creature is named. The rules, for a model with `elbow` only (the heroes, the skeleton, the ogre and the Imp keep the straight arm and the pinned tables; the Chort carries nothing but has the elbow too, for its stance, see `arms.free` under "Carrying"):
- **Carrying.** The forearm of the hand that holds the weapon is held forward of the hanging upper arm by `ELBOW.carry` (blade 0.95 rad, bow 1.2, staff 1.0), an empty hand's by `ELBOW.free` 0.15, or by the description's own `arms.free` (`FigureModel.elbowFree`; the Chort 0.8: both forearms forward, the claws ready in front of the body, so the arms do not hang over the goat legs; an attack bends the arm as any other); a carrying arm keeps `ELBOW.carrySwing` 0.3 of the walk's arm swing. The weapon stands in front of the body in the body's frame (`CARRY`): a blade up and forward out of the fist, a bow upright at its grip with the string towards the body, a staff upright. Blade and bow are turned 0.5 rad about the vertical, so they are edge-on neither from the front nor from the side (the pack's weapon sprites are flat: a blade is a lens `deep` 0.32 thick, a bow two thin rods; seen exactly along their plane they stay a line, which is also what a real blade or bow shows).
- **Attacks.** The upper arm points where the page's pose points the arm; the forearm bends up from it by `ELBOW.swing` 0.3 (swing, slam) or `ELBOW.cast` 0.5; the bow arm is straight. The arms keep their length (`armStretch` is not read).
- **The shot** (`drawArm`). The right hand goes to the line of the shot behind the bow hand, by two-bone reach from the shoulder: at full draw (the only moment the game shows: a shot starts at the release, a wind-up holds it) it stops where that line comes within `ELBOW.anchor` 0.8 of the arm's length of the right shoulder, never nearer the shoulder's own place along the line than `ELBOW.clear` 0.3 (the hand stays in front of the chest), and the elbow points back along the line, a little up and out. A point out of reach straightens the arm towards it. `arms.bowAhead` (`FigureModel.bowAhead`; the goblin) points the bow arm straight ahead from its shoulder instead of crossing to the body's middle line: the goblin's head stands in front of its shoulders, and on the middle line the bow passed through its face.
- **What it cannot do.** The bow's string is a fixed rod of the weapon model: it does not follow the drawing hand, which therefore stands behind the string at full draw. The weapon is hidden while a blade swings (the card's rule above), so the fist is empty in the blow itself.

**The nine** (height in game px, the key in `MODEL_OF_KIND`; the files in `models/descs/`). One human is `HUMAN_HEIGHT` 18 px (`tubeDesc.ts`); each description states its height as a factor of it (`HEIGHT_FACTOR`: Knight 1, robes 1, Orc 1.1, Goblin 0.65, Imp 0.45, Chort 1.05). The robes are drawn to their height (`RobedLook.height`); the others are scaled with `size: fitSize(factor, drawn)`, `drawn` being the height the drawing measured at size 1. Goblin 11.7 (`goblin.ts`, kind `GoblinArcher`); Orc Warrior and Orc Archer 19.8 (`orc.ts`); Necromancer, Summoner and Disciple 18 to the top of the hood, all equally tall (`robed.ts`: one shape, three robes; the body nearly as deep as wide, the hood a cowl fallen back over the head whose line runs from the upper back over the crown to the open front, its point hanging down the back and adding no height; the Disciple has no point); Imp 8.1 (`imp.ts`: slender; bat wings, each one thin flat membrane tube (half thickness 0.36 px, a squared ring, its width following the wing's outline with a scalloped lower edge) with an arm bone, three finger bones and a wrist claw that bend with it; the wings beat across their surface at 5 Hz, 0.9 rad (which the bend's cap levels off at about 0.63), hinged in the first third; slanted glowing yellow eyes without pupils, tubes sunk into the head); Chort 18.9 (`chort.ts`: a heavy horned demon of about human height whose bulk is breadth; it stands crouched and leaning forward, the shoulders in front of the hips, the head low and forward in front of a hump of muscle; a chest more than twice as wide as the waist with a darker painted back; goat legs: the bending joint is the hock, the upper bone holds a thick thigh (forward to the stifle) and the shin (back to the hock), the lower a thin cannon and a dark hoof, `knee: -1`; large bone-white horns on the hat bone that leave the temples sideways and sweep up and back; arms with an elbow and `arms.free` 0.8, three long white claws on each hand; slanted glowing eyes (`DEMON_EYE`) under dark brows, placed high on the head because the camera looks down on it, a dark mouth line with two fangs; a short tail with a spade, kept low: the camera looks down at about 40 degrees, so a long tail stood over the head from the front and hung below the hooves from behind); Knight 18 (`npcKnight.ts`: steel armour with one accent, `KNIGHT_ACCENT` 0x1b3aa0, a deep royal blue chosen for its lightness contrast against the pale steel and not taken from the sprite: the helmet's comb, the mail at the upper arms, the gauntlets, the belt and skirt, the knee cops, the boots and the shield's field; a heater shield on the left arm bone from the shoulder to the hip (`KNIGHT_SHIELD`: the field one flattened tube down the middle whose radius is the half width, so its face is slightly domed, a round steel rim tube along the outline, no rivets; the face is turned 0.5 rad from straight left towards the front, so it is never seen edge-on from the front or the back); the sword it carries is the card's own weapon, `FigureDef.weapon: 'weapon_knight_sword'` of `KIND.Knight` in `game/anim/defs.ts`, not a field of the description (`TubeDesc.weapon` is only a size factor); the steel reads near-white grey, because the shader mixes 20:80 and a per-model mix waits for the user's verdict; dark parts are not `metal`). `hitRadius` of every one is 4.

**Checks.** The thickness check in `tubeModel.test.ts` fails a two-legged description with a tube point whose `r * min(1, wide)` is under 0.35 px (the stag's antler points from #357 are under it, so the check does not cover the animals). `weaponBoneOf(frame)` (`models/bones.ts`) tells which hand carries the weapon, for `figures.ts` and the tests. **The atlas guard:** `build-atlas.ts` fails above 2048 px high. The eight outline frames (one per model key) fit the existing outline strip, which is one row 512 px wide, so the atlas needed no widening: it is 512 × 1984 px (was 1982).
